# Syllabus

The full plan for this library: **25 wings, 255 shelves and 1,866 cards**, from counting to the open problems of current research.

The wings run in order, like a ladder. Each card uses only ideas from cards before it, either earlier in its own wing or in a lower-numbered wing, so the library can be read from the first card to the last. Open a shelf to see its cards; ✅ marks a card that is written and links to it.

**Progress: 1,087 of 1,866 cards written.**

## 01 · Foundations

Work fluently with whole numbers, fractions, decimals, percentages, powers, roots and logarithms; handle compound growth and discounting; read and write logic and proofs; and use sets, relations, functions and the sizes of infinity. This is the toolkit every later wing assumes.

**59 cards in 9 shelves · all written**

<details>

*Whole numbers, the four operations, order of operations, the rearranging laws, negatives, fractions, decimals, ratios, percentages.*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |

| ✅ | [Adding and subtracting: combining and taking away, with carrying and borrowing](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/02-adding-and-subtracting.md) | Add and subtract multi-digit numbers by hand and check with the reverse operation. |
| ✅ | [Multiplying and dividing: repeated adding, fair sharing, and what is left over](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/03-multiplying-and-dividing.md) | Multiply and divide by hand, handle a remainder, and say why dividing by zero has no answer. |
| ✅ | [Order of operations: which step goes first, and why brackets exist](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/04-order-of-operations.md) | Evaluate a mixed expression the way every calculator and spreadsheet does. |

| ✅ | [Decimals: fractions whose bottom is a power of ten](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/08-decimals.md) | Convert between fractions and decimals, line up decimal points, and round money correctly. |

<a name="w02"></a>

## 02 · Number theory

Work with divisibility, primes and the greatest common divisor, compute with remainders on a clock, raise numbers to powers modulo n, and see how check digits, calendars and the codes behind secure messages rest on them.

**43 cards in 7 shelves · all written**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Divisibility and Primes</b> · 8 cards · all written</summary>

*Divides, factors and multiples, parity, digit rules, division with remainder, primes and trial division, the sieve, factor trees, counting divisors. Needs only multiplying-and-dividing and arithmetic-laws (plus place-value and adding-and-subtracting, which precede them). No negatives, no exponents, no proof cards.*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| ✅ | [Divides: when one number goes into another with nothing left over](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/01-divides.md) | Test whether a divides b, list a number's divisors in pairs, and tell a factor from a multiple. |
| ✅ | [Even and odd: the two-way split, and what adding and multiplying do to it](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/02-even-and-odd.md) | Predict the parity of a sum or product without computing it, and use parity to rule a claim out. |
| ✅ | [Divisibility rules: reading the digits to see whether 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9 or 10 divides a number](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/03-divisibility-rules.md) | Apply the digit rules and say why the digit-sum rule for 3 and 9 works (10 is one more than 9). |
| ✅ | [Division with a remainder: the quotient and the leftover are unique, and the leftover is smaller than the divisor](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/04-division-with-remainder.md) | Write any division as b = q x a + r with r below a, and use it as the formal fact behind every later remainder. |
| ✅ | [Primes and composites: the numbers that will not split into equal rows, and the ones that will](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/05-primes-and-composites.md) | Decide by trial division whether a number under 1,000 is prime, stopping once the trial divisor times itself passes the number. |
| ✅ | [The sieve of Eratosthenes: cross out every multiple and the primes are what is left standing](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/06-sieve-of-eratosthenes.md) | Produce all 25 primes under 100 by hand and say why the crossing out stops at 7. |
| ✅ | [Prime factorisation: a factor tree breaks any number into prime atoms, and every route reaches the same atoms](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/07-prime-factorisation.md) | Factor a number into primes by any route and state the fundamental theorem of arithmetic (uniqueness is proved on shelf 2). |

</details>

| :-: | --- | --- |

<a name="w04"></a>

## 04 · Combinatorics and graphs

Count any finite arrangement (orderings, selections, distributions, partitions) by formula and by brute-force listing, and turn a step rule or a counting problem into a recurrence or a generating function and read the answer off. Model a network as a graph and compute or prove the standard facts: degrees, paths and connectivity, trees and cheapest routes, Euler and Hamilton tours, planarity, colourings, matchings and flows, with Ramsey and extremal results in outline.

**82 cards in 14 shelves · all written**

## 05 · Geometry and trig

Measure and reason about shapes in the plane and in space: angles, lengths, areas and volumes by formula and by argument, any triangle from three pieces of data, points and curves by coordinates in three systems, and vectors in space. The wing ends where the flat plane stops being the only option, so that later wings can talk about spheres, curved space and points on a cubic curve.

<a name="w06"></a>

## 06 · Calculus and analysis

Take limits, differentiate and integrate any function met in the library, say exactly when a limit argument is honest, handle series and several variables, and integrate along curves and over surfaces. Rigour and calculation share one route: every rule is proved once, then used.

**70 cards in 9 shelves · all written**

<a name="w07"></a>

## 07 · Complex analysis

Compute with complex numbers, decide when a function is holomorphic, integrate round loops with residues, evaluate real integrals and count zeros by contour, map regions conformally and solve boundary problems on them, and use Fourier and Laplace transforms in outline. Meet the gamma and zeta functions, analytic continuation and the functional equation, and read the route to the Riemann hypothesis that wings 20, 21 and 25 climb.

**60 cards in 9 shelves · all written**

<a name="w08"></a>

## 08 · Differential equations and dynamics

Write down a rate law for a real situation, solve it by hand where a formula exists, step it on a computer where none does, and say whether the answer is trustworthy, stable, oscillating or chaotic. Carry the same toolkit into heat, waves and steady states, into discrete iteration, and into choosing the best path or control, ready for the stochastic and financial versions in wings 11 and 12.

**93 cards in 12 shelves · all written**

Model chance with the standard distributions, compute and simulate probabilities, estimate from data with honest error bars, test claims, fit regressions and time series, and say what a result does and does not show. Proofs of the limit theorems are in wing 10; this wing states them and uses them.

<a name="w10"></a>

## 10 · Measure and integration

Say which sets have a size, build Lebesgue measure and the Lebesgue integral from scratch, swap limits and integrals with the right theorem, integrate on products, change measure with a density, and condition on a sigma-algebra. Probability is rebuilt on that footing: random variables as measurable maps, expectation as an integral, conditional expectation, modes of convergence, and the laws of large numbers and central limit theorem proved.

**68 cards in 11 shelves · all written**

## 11 · Stochastic processes and calculus

Follow randomness through time: random walks, martingales, Markov chains, Poisson arrivals, Brownian motion and the calculus built on it. Write and solve a stochastic differential equation, change the probability measure, simulate a path honestly, and state the theorems the finance wing rests on.

<a name="w12"></a>

## 12 · Financial mathematics

Price, hedge, risk-manage and solve backwards the main products across rates, equity, FX, commodities, credit and their combinations, plus portfolio, risk, trading and insurance mathematics. Each formula is solved for each of its inputs where that is possible, and the card says when it is not and on what domain.

**332 cards in 51 shelves · all written**

| ✅ | [Seasonal curves: natural gas forwards that hump every winter, and the storage trade that keeps summer-to-winter spreads bounded](Cards/12-Financial%20mathematics/25-Commodity%20forwards%20-%20carry%2C%20storage%2C%20convenience%20yield%20and%20the%20curve/05-seasonality-and-the-gas-curve.md) | Model the forward curve as a deterministic seasonal shape times a level, fit the shape from a strip, bound the summer-winter spread by injection, storage and withdrawal costs plus interest, and see why the bound only works one way. |

Design and tune a controller, process a sampled signal, estimate hidden states, and read the equations of circuits, structures, fluids, heat and quantum mechanics well enough to follow an engineering text.

| ✅ | [The z-transform: difference equations become algebra in the z-plane](Cards/13-Engineering%20mathematics/02-Linear%20Systems%20and%20Transforms/08-z-transform-and-discrete-time-systems.md) | Transform a difference equation, place its poles in the z-plane, and test stability against the unit circle. |
| ✅ | [Discretising a design: hold the input flat, or bend the frequency axis](Cards/13-Engineering%20mathematics/02-Linear%20Systems%20and%20Transforms/09-zero-order-hold-and-tustin-discretisation.md) | Convert a continuous model to a sampled one exactly by zero-order hold and approximately by Tustin, then compare. |

</details>

<summary><b>04 · State Space and Optimal Control</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Many states at once: simulate, test what you can steer and see, place poles, optimise and estimate*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |

| · | Linearisation: near a chosen operating point a curved model becomes a straight one | Find an equilibrium, take the Jacobians, and write the small-disturbance model every linear tool then applies to. |
| · | Can you steer every state, and can you see every state | Run both rank tests on the cart and pendulum, price the hard directions with the Gramians, and use duality once. |
| · | Pole placement: if you can steer it, you can choose its response speeds | Place closed-loop poles by state feedback, build a Luenberger observer by duality, and run the two together. |
| · | Stability of a state-space model: eigenvalue tests and the energy that certifies them | Test a continuous and a sampled model by eigenvalues, then solve the Lyapunov equation for the certifying energy. |
| · | The LQR: choose feedback by pricing error against control effort | Write the quadratic cost, solve the Riccati equation, read the gain, and watch the weights trade speed for effort. |
| · | The Kalman filter: predict forward, then correct by the measurement's surprise | Run the predict-correct recursion on the cart and pendulum, derive the gain, and check the surprises look like noise. |
| · | Filtering a nonlinear plant: relinearise each step, or push sample points through | Run an extended and an unscented filter on a wide swing, tune the two covariances, and spot divergence. |
| · | LQG: design regulator and estimator apart, then bolt them together | Combine the regulator with the filter, justify it by the separation principle, and see the robustness it can lose. |
| · | Predictive control: plan a whole future, use the first move, replan next tick | Turn a constrained prediction into a quadratic program, apply only the first move, and add a disturbance state for zero offset. |

| | Card | You'll be able to |

| · | Sampling: measure faster than twice the top frequency or fast becomes slow | State and use the sampling theorem, predict an aliased frequency, and size the filter that goes before the converter. |
| · | The DFT and the FFT: a block of samples becomes a list of frequencies | Compute a DFT, get bin width from record length, and see how halving again and again gives the fast transform. |
| · | Windows: a recording has to stop, so energy leaks between frequencies | Explain leakage from the plain window, choose a taper for a job, and trade peak width against sidelobe level. |

| · | Changing rate: filter then drop samples, or pad with zeros then smooth | Decimate and interpolate a recording by an integer factor, then convert 48 kilohertz to 44.1 without aliases or images. |

| · | Wiener and LMS: the best linear guess, then learning it sample by sample | Derive the Wiener filter from the orthogonality principle and run least mean squares to cancel a measured noise source. |
| · | Wavelets: a coarse picture plus the detail you left out at every scale | Run a Haar transform by hand, climb the multiresolution ladder, and denoise by shrinking small coefficients. |
| · | Spectrograms: one spectrum per slice of time, and the blur you cannot avoid | Build a spectrogram from overlapping windows and read the time against frequency trade-off straight off the picture. |
| · | Compressed sensing: few measurements suffice when the signal is mostly zeros | Set up a sparse recovery problem, swap counting terms for summing sizes, and recover a sparse spectrum from random samples. |

| · | Phasors: steady AC becomes complex arithmetic, and impedance replaces resistance | Convert an AC circuit to impedances, solve it as one complex division, and read amplitude and phase off the answer. |
| · | RLC resonance: one frequency the circuit answers loudest, and how sharp the peak is | Find resonant frequency and quality factor for a series RLC tuner and match them to the spring and mass analogy. |

| · | Magnetic fields: currents make them, moving charges feel them, change induces voltage | Compute the field inside a solenoid, its inductance, and the voltage induced when the current is switched off. |

| · | Electromagnetic waves: the two curl equations feed each other and light falls out | Derive the wave equation from Maxwell in vacuum, get the speed from two constants, and read wavelength and polarisation. |

| · | Vibration modes: a structure rings in a few shapes at a few frequencies | Solve the generalised eigenvalue problem for a two-mass frame and match a driving force to its worst mode. |

| · | Navier-Stokes: Newton's law for a fluid parcel, and the number that says which term wins | Assemble the momentum equation term by term, nondimensionalise it, and read the Reynolds number off the result. |

| · | Fourier's law: heat runs down a temperature gradient, and the heat equation follows | Compute steady loss through a layered wall, then set up the transient heat equation with its boundary conditions. |
| · | Convection and radiation: the other two ways heat leaves a surface | Add a convection coefficient and a fourth-power radiation term to one surface balance and compare their sizes. |
| · | The thermodynamic laws: energy is conserved and entropy only grows | Apply the first law to a closed system and use entropy to rule out the processes that cannot happen. |
| · | Heat engines: the best efficiency anything between two temperatures can reach | Compute Carnot efficiency and a heat pump's coefficient of performance, and place a real cycle below the limit. |

| · | Adapting as you go: schedule the gains, or let the controller learn the plant | Schedule a controller across operating points, then run recursive least squares inside a model-reference adaptive loop. |

---

<a name="w14"></a>

## 14 · Applied and computational

Analyse an algorithm's cost and choose a data structure, use randomness and approximation on purpose, measure information in bits and code it for storage and for a noisy channel, follow modern cryptography from the one-time pad to lattices, formulate an operations-research model and read a solver's answer, do the mathematics behind machine learning, and analyse a network as a matrix. Theory that belongs elsewhere is named and linked rather than half-taught: algorithms and duality for optimisation in wing 15, method derivations and error bounds in wing 16, complexity classes in wing 24.

**71 cards in 8 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Algorithms and Growth</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*How cost grows, which case you are quoting, and the workhorse patterns: divide and conquer, sorting, structures, tables, greed*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Big-O: the growth rate of a cost, not its stopwatch reading | Read and write O, Omega and Theta, and rank constant, log, linear, n log n, quadratic and exponential costs. |
| · | Which case are you quoting? Worst, average and amortised cost | Tell worst from average case and quote an amortised cost for a structure that occasionally does something expensive. |
| · | Polynomial or exponential: the line between doable and hopeless | Compare n cubed with 2 to the n at real sizes, and say why an exponential search must be replaced rather than tuned. |
| · | Recurrences: the cost of a method that calls itself, read off in one line | Write T(n) for a divide-and-conquer method and get its order from the master theorem, checking with a recursion tree. |
| · | Sorting and searching: merge sort, quicksort, binary search and the n log n floor | Sort by merging and by partitioning, search a sorted list by halving, and prove no comparison sort beats n log n. |
| · | Data structures: what each one makes cheap, and what it makes dear | Choose between array, linked list, stack, queue, hash table, binary heap and balanced tree from the operations you need. |
| · | Dynamic programming: solve each small piece once and write the answer down | Spot optimal substructure and overlapping subproblems, then fill a table forwards or memoise backwards. |
| · | Greedy: take the best next step, and the argument that it is safe | Run a greedy choice, prove it optimal by an exchange or staying-ahead argument, and show a case where greed fails. |
| · | Graph algorithms as code: the structures that make wing 04's theorems run fast | Implement breadth-first search, Dijkstra with a heap, Kruskal with union-find, Tarjan, A star and Dinic, and quote each cost. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · Randomised and Approximate Algorithms</b> · 8 cards</summary>

*Randomness as a design tool: expectation, tail bounds, sketches, guaranteed ratios, and sampling used as computation*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Randomised algorithms: coin flips inside the method, and the expected cost | Analyse expected running time by linearity of expectation, and tell a Monte Carlo answer from a Las Vegas one. |
| · | Tail bounds: why a random method almost never embarrasses you | Bound the failure chance with Markov, Chebyshev, Chernoff and the union bound, then choose how many repeats to run. |
| · | Fair choices from a list you cannot hold: Fisher-Yates and reservoir sampling | Shuffle in place so every order is equally likely, and keep a uniform sample of k items from an endless stream. |
| · | Hashing for size: Bloom filters and sketches that trade certainty for memory | Size a Bloom filter for a target false-positive rate, and estimate a distinct count from a small sketch. |
| · | Monte Carlo integration: random points beat a grid once the dimension climbs | Estimate an integral by sampling, quote the one-over-root-N error, and show why a grid dies in ten dimensions. |
| · | Approximation: a guaranteed ratio when the exact answer is out of reach | Prove a 2-approximation for vertex cover and a log-factor one for set cover, and round a fractional solution. |
| · | Local search: hill climbing, restarts, annealing and genetic moves | Design a neighbourhood and a move, escape a local optimum by annealing or restart, and report the result honestly. |
| · | MCMC as a tool: Metropolis-Hastings when you cannot sample directly | Build a proposal, accept by the Metropolis ratio, diagnose burn-in and mixing, and estimate from the chain. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>03 · Information Theory</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Measuring information in bits, compressing it, and pushing it through a channel that makes mistakes*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Entropy: the average number of yes-or-no questions an outcome costs | Compute entropy in bits for a discrete source, read the binary entropy curve, and see that uniform is the maximum. |
| · | Conditional entropy and mutual information: how much one signal tells you about another | Compute H(Y given X) and I(X;Y), use the chain rule, and show that conditioning never increases entropy. |
| · | KL divergence: the bits you waste by coding for the wrong distribution | Compute KL and cross-entropy, prove KL is never negative by Gibbs, and read maximum likelihood as divergence minimisation. |
| · | Typical sequences: long messages all look alike, and that is where the limit comes from | State the asymptotic equipartition property, count the typical set, and read entropy as bits per symbol. |
| · | Huffman coding: the shortest prefix-free code, built from the bottom up | Build a Huffman tree by hand, check Kraft-McMillan, and compare the code length against the entropy bound. |
| · | Arithmetic and Lempel-Ziv coding: beating Huffman on fractions and on repetition | Encode a message as one interval to fractional bits, build an LZ dictionary from repeats, and name the formats that use each. |
| · | Channel capacity: the fastest error-free rate a noisy link will allow | Compute capacity for a binary symmetric channel and by Shannon-Hartley, and state the coding theorem and its converse. |
| · | Hamming and Reed-Solomon: codes that repair what the channel broke | Encode and syndrome-decode Hamming (7,4), read minimum distance as a repair budget, and outline Reed-Solomon over a finite field. |
| · | LDPC, turbo and polar codes: how modern links get within a whisker of capacity | Read a Tanner graph, follow belief propagation and Viterbi decoding in outline, and say where each code is used today. |
| · | Rate and distortion: how many bits for a picture you can live with | State the rate-distortion function, work the Gaussian case, and follow transform coding and quantisation through a photo. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>04 · Cryptography</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*What each primitive promises, what breaks it, and the arithmetic underneath: pads, block ciphers, hashes, RSA, curves, lattices, proofs*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The one-time pad: perfect secrecy, and the price that makes it impractical | Prove the pad leaks nothing, see why a key must never be reused, and state Kerckhoffs's principle. |
| · | AES: a block cipher as a keyed shuffle, and the modes that use it safely | Follow one AES round (substitute, shift, mix, add key), choose a mode with a fresh nonce, and use authenticated encryption. |
| · | Hashes and MACs: a fingerprint for data, and a tag only a key could make | State collision, preimage and second-preimage resistance, compute the birthday bound, and build HMAC and a password KDF. |
| · | RSA in full: key generation, padding, and why factoring is the whole game | Generate a keypair with Euler's theorem, encrypt with OAEP, speed decryption with the remainder theorem, and break textbook RSA. |
| · | Diffie-Hellman and ElGamal: agreeing a secret in public, then encrypting with it | Run an exchange in a prime-order group, state the discrete-log and Diffie-Hellman assumptions, and encrypt with ElGamal. |
| · | Elliptic curves: the same protocols with 256-bit keys instead of 3,072 | Add points on a curve over a prime field, multiply by double-and-add, and run ECDH and ECDSA end to end. |
| · | Digital signatures: hash then sign, and what a forgery would have to mean | Sign and verify by hash-and-sign, state unforgeability under chosen-message attack, and show how one reused nonce leaks a key. |
| · | The TLS handshake: every primitive in one short conversation | Trace a handshake through hybrid KEM-DEM encryption, certificate checking and forward secrecy, and see where a man in the middle is stopped. |
| · | Lattices: hard problems a quantum computer does not break, and the new standards | State the shortest-vector and learning-with-errors problems, follow Kyber and Dilithium in outline, and say what Shor's algorithm ends. |
| · | Zero-knowledge: convincing someone you know a secret without showing it | Run a sigma protocol for a discrete log, check completeness, soundness and the simulator, and go non-interactive by Fiat-Shamir. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>05 · Operations Research</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Writing a decision as a model a solver can answer, then reading the answer: programs, schedules, stock, queues, decisions, games*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Linear programs: a plan written as variables, one target and straight-line limits | Write objective and constraints in standard form, see why the best answer sits at a corner, and call a solver. |
| · | Shadow prices: what one more oven hour is worth, straight off the solver | Read dual values and sensitivity ranges from a solver report, and say which constraint to relax first and by how much. |
| · | Integer programs: yes-or-no decisions, and the search tree that settles them | Model with binary variables, bound with the fractional relaxation, branch on a fraction, and quote the integrality gap. |
| · | Scheduling: the critical path, the float, and rules that beat a gut feeling | Build a project network, compute earliest and latest starts, find the critical path and float, and apply the SPT and EDD rules. |
| · | The assignment problem: pairing people to jobs at least total cost | Write the assignment as a program or a matching, and solve a 4 by 4 cost matrix by the Hungarian method. |
| · | Stock: the order size that balances holding against ordering, and the one-shot order | Derive the economic order quantity, set a reorder point and safety stock from lead-time demand, and use the critical fractile. |
| · | Queues: Little's law, M/M/1, and why ninety percent busy feels broken | Compute utilisation, queue length and waiting time for M/M/1 and M/M/c, and apply Little's law to any stable system. |
| · | Deciding under uncertainty: expected utility, and what a perfect forecast is worth | Build a decision tree, choose by expected utility rather than expected money, and price perfect information. |
| · | Nash equilibrium: nobody gains by moving alone, in pure or mixed strategies | Read a payoff matrix, find best responses and pure equilibria, and solve a 2 by 2 game for its mixed equilibrium. |
| · | Auctions: why a second-price rule makes honesty the best policy | Compare first-price and Vickrey auctions, prove truthful bidding is optimal in Vickrey, and state revenue equivalence. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>06 · Machine Learning Mathematics</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*The mathematics under the libraries: a loss to minimise, a gradient to follow, and the linear algebra behind the rest*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Loss and empirical risk: writing "learning" as a sum you can minimise | Choose squared, absolute, hinge or log loss, write the empirical risk, and keep training error apart from test error. |
| · | Gradient descent: walk downhill, and take a noisy step when the data is large | Derive the update, choose a step size, run mini-batch stochastic descent, and see momentum and convergence in outline. |
| · | The perceptron and its stack: weights, a bias, a squashing function, layers | Train a perceptron on separable data, show what one layer cannot do, and assemble a two-layer network. |
| · | Backpropagation: the chain rule run backwards through a network | Compute gradients layer by layer by the chain rule, and check one weight's derivative against a numerical difference. |
| · | Softmax and cross-entropy: scores into probabilities, then scored in bits | Turn scores into probabilities with softmax, minimise cross-entropy, and link it back to logistic regression and KL divergence. |
| · | k-means: groups defined by their own centres, found by taking turns | Run assign-and-update to convergence, watch the objective fall, and choose k by an elbow or a silhouette. |
| · | Principal components: the few directions that carry most of the variation | Compute components from the covariance matrix or the SVD, read the variance explained, and project to two dimensions to plot. |
| · | EM: guess the hidden labels, refit, repeat until nothing moves | Run the E and M steps for a two-component mixture, watch the likelihood climb, and name where the method gets stuck. |
| · | Support vector machines: the widest safe margin, and the kernel trick | Find the maximum-margin boundary, allow slack for overlap, and replace dot products by a kernel to bend the boundary. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>07 · Network Science and Spectral Graphs</b> · 8 cards</summary>

*Networks as matrices: who matters, where the groups are, and how fast news, a virus or a random walker spreads*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Centrality: degree, betweenness and PageRank as rival ideas of "important" | Compute degree, closeness and betweenness centrality, and define PageRank as a random surfer's long-run share. |
| · | PageRank by power iteration: multiply, normalise, repeat, and why damping is needed | Run power iteration on a sparse adjacency matrix, handle dangling pages with damping, and watch the error shrink. |
| · | The graph Laplacian: eigenvalues that count the pieces and cut the network in two | Build L as degree minus adjacency, read zero eigenvalues as components, and split a network with the Fiedler vector. |
| · | Small worlds and hubs: three models, and the real networks they imitate | Compare Erdos-Renyi, Watts-Strogatz and preferential attachment on degree distribution, clustering and path length. |
| · | Epidemics on a network: the threshold, and why the hubs decide the outcome | Run an SIR model on a network, get the threshold from the degree distribution, and compare hub-first with random vaccination. |
| · | Communities: modularity, and the algorithms that chase it | Define modularity, run a greedy merge to a partition, and test the answer against a network with planted groups. |
| · | Expanders: sparse networks that are hard to cut and quick to mix | Define edge expansion and the spectral gap, bound mixing time by the gap, and say why expanders make good samplers. |
| · | Random walks as circuits: hitting times, resistance and commute times | Turn a graph into resistors, compute effective resistance, and read hitting and commute times off the circuit. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>08 · Scientific Computing Practice</b> · 7 cards</summary>

*How the library keeps its numbers honest: floating point, seeds, cost, tolerances, two independent roads, and asserts that can fail*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Floating point: why 0.1 plus 0.2 is not 0.3, and what the machine does promise | Read an IEEE 754 double, use machine epsilon and relative error, and predict where rounding will show up. |
| · | Cancellation: subtracting near-equal numbers, and the rewrite that saves the digits | Spot cancellation, rewrite the quadratic formula and a variance sum, and total a long list by Kahan summation. |
| · | Seeds: a random result that anyone can reproduce, line for line | Seed every generator explicitly, record the seed beside the answer, and give independent parts independent streams. |
| · | Cost in practice: count the operations, then let arrays do them at once | Count the operations in a loop, rewrite it as array work in Python, and compare against a plain Rust loop. |
| · | Tolerances: never test two computed numbers for equality | Choose an absolute and a relative tolerance for a comparison, and set the tolerance each card's checks will use. |
| · | Two roads and a mutation: a test that would notice if the answer were wrong | Get every number by two independent methods, then break the code on purpose and confirm the test fails. |
| · | The check script: asserts that can fail, run on every card | Write one runnable script whose asserts reproduce every number in a card, and make it exit non-zero when one breaks. |

</details>

<a name="w15"></a>

## 15 · Optimization

Recognise a convex problem, prove the optimality conditions that certify its answer, and run gradient, Newton, quasi-Newton, trust region, simplex, interior point and proximal methods knowing how fast each converges. Use duality to bound and certify an answer, handle integer, cone and uncertain problems, and set up a dynamic program over time.

**64 cards in 7 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Convexity</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Convex sets and convex functions, the tests that certify them, and the separation and conjugacy facts every later shelf rests on*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Local and global minima: the dip nearby against the true bottom | Distinguish local from global minima, and show why a general search can only certify a local one. |
| · | Convex sets: shapes with no dents, where the straight line stays inside | Test a set for convexity, build the convex hull of a few points, and recognise a polyhedron. |
| · | Convex functions: chords above the graph, and the curvature test that proves it | Check convexity from the chord definition, the gradient inequality, and a positive semidefinite Hessian. |
| · | Convexity pays off: every local bottom of a convex problem is the lowest | Prove that a local minimum of a convex function on a convex set is global, and that the minimiser set is convex. |
| · | Jensen's inequality: averaging inside a bowl beats averaging outside | Prove Jensen from supporting lines and use it to bound an average cost. |
| · | Separating hyperplanes: a flat wall between a point and a convex set it misses | Prove the separating hyperplane theorem in finite dimensions and read Farkas' lemma off it. |
| · | Subgradients: slopes that still work where the graph has a kink | Compute the subdifferential of the absolute value and of a max, and state the zero-subgradient optimality rule. |
| · | Convex conjugates: describing a function by its slopes instead of its values | Compute conjugates of a quadratic and of an absolute value, then state Fenchel-Young and Fenchel duality. |
| · | Building convex functions: the operations that cannot break the bowl | Certify a newly built objective as convex using sums, maxima, compositions and partial minimisation. |
| · | The standard convex problem: the shape every solver expects | Write a problem in standard convex form and check each piece against the rules a solver enforces. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · Unconstrained Methods</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Conditions for a minimum, then the standard descent algorithms and an honest account of how fast each one gets there*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Optimality conditions: flat slope first, upward curvature second | Find stationary points, classify them with the Hessian, and state the second-order sufficient condition. |
| · | Gradient descent: step downhill, and how the shape of the valley sets the pace | Implement gradient descent with a fixed step, prove descent for a smooth function, and see zigzagging in a stretched bowl. |
| · | Convergence rates: linear, superlinear, quadratic, and what stretch costs you | Measure a method's rate from its error sequence and predict the slowdown from the condition number. |
| · | Line search: how far to go once you have picked a direction | Implement backtracking with the Armijo test, state the Wolfe conditions, and show why curvature matters for quasi-Newton. |
| · | Newton and BFGS: use curvature, measured or remembered | Implement Newton's step with a Hessian modification, then BFGS building curvature from past steps. |
| · | L-BFGS: quasi-Newton when there are a million variables | Store only the last few step pairs, reconstruct the search direction, and weigh the memory against the accuracy. |
| · | Conjugate gradient: directions that never undo each other's work | Derive the conjugate directions for a quadratic, solve one in n steps, then run the nonlinear version. |
| · | Trust regions: trust the model only as far as it has earned | Solve the trust region subproblem approximately, apply the ratio test, and resize the region. |
| · | Nonlinear least squares: Gauss-Newton, and the damping that rescues it | Form the Jacobian, take a Gauss-Newton step, and add Levenberg-Marquardt damping when it diverges. |
| · | Automatic differentiation: exact derivatives from the code, not from small steps | Differentiate a short program in forward and reverse mode by hand, then check against a finite difference. |

</details>

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Lagrange multipliers, proved: why the gradients must line up | Prove the multiplier theorem from the implicit function theorem and read each multiplier as a price. |
| · | KKT conditions: the checklist when inequalities can bind or slacken | State and verify all four KKT conditions on a small problem, and see where a constraint qualification is needed. |
| · | Second-order conditions under constraints: curvature on the allowed directions only | Build the critical cone and test the Lagrangian Hessian on it to confirm a constrained minimum. |
| · | Lagrangian duality: pricing the constraints gives a bound, sometimes exactly | Form the dual function, prove weak duality and its concavity, and state when strong duality holds. |
| · | Slater's condition: the strict feasibility that closes the gap | Check Slater's condition, compute a duality gap in a nonconvex example, and use a zero gap as a stopping certificate. |
| · | Saddle points: the same problem read as a two-player minimax | Recast the Lagrangian as a minimax game and state the minimax theorem that lets you swap the order. |
| · | Penalties and augmented Lagrangians: fines that need not grow to infinity | Solve a constrained problem by a growing quadratic penalty, then cure its ill-conditioning with multiplier updates. |
| · | Interior point methods: a barrier that keeps you strictly inside | Add a log barrier, trace the central path as the barrier weakens, and relate that path to the KKT system. |
| · | Sequential quadratic programming: a quadratic model of the constrained problem each step | Build and solve the SQP subproblem, add a merit function, and note the Maratos effect. |

</details>

| · | The simplex method: walk the corners, improving on every move | Run simplex by hand with a tableau, pick entering and leaving variables, and handle a degenerate pivot. |
| · | LP duality: the partner problem with the same value, and the slackness that pairs them | State and prove weak and strong LP duality, then verify complementary slackness on the workshop problem. |
| · | Shadow prices: what one more hour of labour is actually worth | Read shadow prices and reduced costs off the final tableau and give the range over which each stays valid. |
| · | Network flows: shipping problems that are linear programs with whole answers | Write a transportation problem as an LP, solve it, and see why its optimum comes out integral. |
| · | Modelling tricks: absolute values, worst cases and kinked costs as linear programs | Convert an absolute-value, a minimax and a piecewise-linear cost into linear programs with extra variables. |
| · | Interior point for linear programs: polynomial time, and when simplex still wins | Run a primal-dual interior point step on the workshop LP and compare its worst case with simplex's. |

</details>

| · | Yes-or-no variables: switching constraints on and off with big-M | Model either-or, if-then and fixed-charge conditions with binaries, and choose M without wrecking the relaxation. |
| · | Branch and bound: split into cases and throw away what cannot win | Run branch and bound by hand on a small integer program, recording bounds and pruned nodes. |
| · | Cutting planes: slicing off fractional corners without losing integer points | Derive a Gomory cut from a fractional tableau row and add it, then see branch and cut combine both ideas. |
| · | Total unimodularity: the matrices whose linear programs are already integral | Check a matrix for total unimodularity and explain why network and assignment problems need no branching. |
| · | Four classic models: knapsack, assignment, covering and routing | Formulate each of the four standard integer models and recognise which one a new problem really is. |
| · | Lagrangian relaxation: price the awkward constraint and solve what is left | Relax a coupling constraint into the objective, solve the dual by subgradient steps, and compare that bound with the LP one. |
| · | Heuristics with a promise: greedy bounds and approximation ratios | State and prove a greedy approximation ratio, then improve a tour with two-opt moves. |

</details>

| · | Two goals at once: Pareto optimality and the frontier of trade-offs | Define Pareto optimality, trace a frontier by weighted sums, and show which points weighting can never reach. |
| · | Support vector machines: the widest gap as a quadratic program | Write the maximum-margin classifier as a QP, take its dual, and read the support vectors off the multipliers. |
| · | Second-order cone programs: constraints shaped like ice cream cones | Recognise cone-representable constraints, convert a norm bound into one, and state conic duality. |

<a name="w16"></a>

## 16 · Numerical analysis

Say how accurate a computed number is and why, and choose, cost and analyse a method for roots, linear systems, eigenvalues, interpolation, integrals, ODEs and PDEs. Every convergence claim the practical wings leaned on is proved here, with its failure mode and its price in arithmetic named.

**68 cards in 8 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Floating Point and Error</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*How a machine holds a number, where digits die, how much the problem itself magnifies a wobble, and what the work costs*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Absolute and relative error: wrong by how much, wrong by what fraction | Quote an error both ways, convert between them, and count the correct significant digits in a result. |
| · | IEEE 754: sign, exponent and fraction, and the numbers a computer cannot hold | Read a double's three fields, explain why one tenth is inexact, and handle infinities, NaN and subnormals without surprise. |
| · | Machine epsilon and cancellation: the gap next to one, and digits subtraction destroys | Bound a single rounding with the standard model, spot catastrophic cancellation, and rewrite a formula that suffers from it. |
| · | Two different errors: the formula's truncation and the machine's rounding | Separate error made by the approximation from error made by the arithmetic, and see why shrinking a step stops helping. |
| · | Conditioning and stability: a fragile problem is not the same thing as a sloppy method | Compute a condition number, tell an ill-conditioned problem from an unstable algorithm, and say which one your trouble is. |
| · | Forward and backward error: a wrong answer, or the exact answer to a wrong question | Compute both errors for a computed root and use the rule of thumb that ties them through the condition number. |
| · | Adding a million numbers without losing the small ones: Kahan, pairwise and Welford | Bound naive summation error, run compensated and pairwise summation, and keep a mean and variance in one pass. |
| · | Counting the work: flops, growth rates, and why a tuned library beats your loop | Count the operations a method needs, compare growth at a realistic size, and say when memory traffic rather than arithmetic decides. |
| · | Interval arithmetic: carry a guaranteed range instead of a single number | Add and multiply intervals, read the guaranteed enclosure, and see why intervals widen too fast for long computations. |

</details>

| · | Newton and secant: a tangent step and a chord step, and how fast each converges | Prove Newton doubles the correct digits near a simple root and that the secant method's order is the golden ratio. |

<a name="w17"></a>

## 17 · Topology

Work in a metric or an abstract topological space, and prove the compactness and connectedness facts that every analysis wing has been leaning on. Classify a closed surface by hand, compute a fundamental group and a homology group on a concrete example, and run persistent homology on real data while saying honestly what it is worth.

**51 cards in 6 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Metric Spaces</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*The three distance rules, the vocabulary they generate, and the four theorems that need completeness*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Metric space: the three rules that make a distance mean something | Check the three axioms on a candidate distance and use the triangle inequality to bound a gap you cannot measure. |
| · | The distance menu: straight-line, taxicab, worst-case, and distance between whole shapes | Pick a metric for vectors, functions, graphs or sets, and say when two metrics give the same open sets. |
| · | Open balls, open sets and closed sets: the vocabulary a distance hands you | Decide whether a set is open, closed, both or neither, and build any open set from balls. |
| · | Limits and continuity with distances: epsilon and delta in any metric space | Prove a sequence converges in a given metric and show a map is continuous by epsilon-delta or by sequences. |
| · | Complete space: bunched-up sequences land somewhere, and how to fill in the gaps | Test a space for completeness and build a completion, the way the reals complete the rationals. |
| · | Compactness three ways: finite covers, convergent subsequences, closed and bounded | Use Heine-Borel in n dimensions, extract a convergent subsequence, and see why the three versions agree here. |
| · | Uniform continuity: one tolerance for the whole space, and free on a compact set | Tell pointwise from uniform continuity in a metric space, prove Heine-Cantor, and use a Lipschitz constant. |
| · | Banach's fixed point theorem: a shrinking map on a complete space has one fixed point | Verify a contraction constant, iterate to the fixed point, and bound the error left after n steps. |
| · | Arzela-Ascoli: when a whole family of functions has a convergent subsequence | Check pointwise boundedness and equicontinuity, then extract a uniformly convergent subsequence and name its limit. |
| · | Baire category theorem: a complete space is never a countable pile of thin sets | Define nowhere dense and meagre, prove the theorem, and use it to rule out one countable decomposition. |

</details>

*Nearness with no distances at all, the three standard constructions, and the separation and compactness theorems*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Topological space: you name the open sets, and a basis generates them | Check the three axioms for a topology, generate one from a basis, and compare two topologies for fineness. |
| · | Closure, interior and boundary: the solid inside, the edge, and everything a set touches | Compute the three for a set in a given topology and use dense sets and limit points with no metric available. |
| · | Continuity by pre-images, and homeomorphism: when two shapes are the same for topology | Prove a map continuous by pre-images, glue continuous pieces, and exhibit a homeomorphism with its inverse. |
| · | Subspace, product and quotient: cutting a piece out, pairing up, gluing points together | Put the right topology on a subset, a product and a quotient, and recognise the circle and torus as each. |
| · | Hausdorff and its ladder: how far apart open sets can pry two points | Check the Hausdorff condition, see where it makes limits unique, and place regular and normal on the ladder. |
| · | Compactness with no distances, and Tychonoff: any product of compact spaces is compact | Argue with open covers and the finite intersection property in a general space, and state where choice enters. |
| · | Connected and path-connected: one piece, and one piece you can walk across | Split a space into components, prove an interval is connected, and give a connected space with no paths in it. |
| · | What compactness and connectedness buy: attained maxima, crossed values, free inverses | Prove continuous images keep both properties, then read off the extreme and intermediate value theorems. |
| · | The test spaces: discrete, indiscrete, cofinite, the Cantor set, and what each one breaks | Use a small example space to refute a general claim, and read the Cantor set as compact and totally disconnected. |
| · | Urysohn and Tietze: a dial between closed sets, and when a space is secretly metric | Build a continuous dial separating two closed sets, extend a function off a closed piece, and state metrisation. |

</details>

| | Card | You'll be able to |

| · | Contractible and simply connected: no holes at all, and every loop can be shrunk | Decide which of a disc, an annulus and a punctured plane are contractible or simply connected, and say why. |
| · | The fundamental group: loops at a point, multiplied by running one after the other | Build the group from loop classes, check the group axioms, and show a continuous map induces a homomorphism. |

| · | Brouwer and Borsuk-Ulam: a disc map fixes a point, and antipodes give equal readings | Prove Brouwer in the plane from the no-retraction theorem, and state Borsuk-Ulam with its sandwich corollary. |

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |

| · | Homology groups: cycles that are not boundaries, one hole count per dimension | Compute H0 and H1 of a triangulated annulus as cycles modulo boundaries, and read off the Betti numbers. |
| · | Smith normal form: the matrix routine that turns boundary matrices into hole counts | Reduce the boundary matrices over the integers, read the Betti numbers, and spot torsion when it appears. |
| · | Homotopy invariance: spaces you can deform into each other have identical hole counts | Use invariance to get the homology of a solid ball and of a deformation retract without any new computation. |
| · | Cellular homology: hole counts read straight off the gluing recipe, quick by hand | Compute the homology of the torus, the Klein bottle and the projective plane from their cell structures. |
| · | Mayer-Vietoris and exact sequences: the holes of a whole from two overlapping pieces | Read an exact sequence, get the sphere's homology from two caps, and say what relative homology ignores. |
| · | Homology of spheres, and degree: how many times one sphere wraps around another | Compute the homology of every sphere, define the degree of a self-map, and count preimages with it. |
| · | Cohomology and the cup product in outline: the dual view, carrying a multiplication | Say how cochains dualise chains, state Poincare duality on a closed orientable surface, and see what the product separates. |

| · | Vietoris-Rips, Cech and alpha complexes: three ways to make a shape out of points | Build the Rips and Cech complexes at one radius, compare their sizes, and state the nerve theorem. |

| · | Persistence diagram and stability: noise in the data moves the picture only a little | Plot births against deaths, judge a feature by its distance from the diagonal, and state the bottleneck bound. |
| · | Comparing diagrams: bottleneck distance, landscapes, images, and honest error bars | Compute a bottleneck distance between two diagrams, turn one into a fixed-length vector, and bootstrap a band. |
| · | Mapper and Reeb graphs: a network summary of a dataset along a chosen lens | Build a Mapper graph from a lens, a cover and a clustering, and read it as an approximate Reeb graph. |
| · | Persistence in practice: the shape of a time series, and what that shape is worth | Embed a series by sliding windows, compute its persistence, and judge whether the feature survives noise and resampling. |

</details>

<a name="w18"></a>

## 18 · Functional analysis

Work in infinite-dimensional spaces as steadily as in ordinary space: measure size with a norm, rely on completeness, name the dual, and read an operator through its spectrum. Differentiate kinks and spikes, pose an equation weakly in a Sobolev space, and turn a generator into an evolution that runs forward in time.

**56 cards in 6 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Normed and Banach Spaces</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*length and completeness in spaces of sequences and functions; the three Banach theorems; Hahn-Banach and the dual space*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Normed and Banach spaces: giving a length to functions so limits cannot escape | Check the three norm rules on a space of functions and say what completeness buys. |
| · | Sequence spaces: infinite lists of numbers with a size rule | Compute norms in l1, l2, sup and c0, and say which sequences belong to which space. |
| · | Function spaces C and Lp: two ways to size a function, two different worlds | Compare sup-norm and averaged convergence on the same functions, and pick the right space. |
| · | Equivalent norms: in finitely many dimensions every length rule agrees | Show all norms on n coordinates give the same limits, then watch the claim fail for functions. |
| · | Infinite dimensions: the unit ball stops being compact and bases get subtler | Explain why a bounded sequence can have no convergent subsequence, and what replaces a basis. |
| · | Hahn-Banach: a measurement on part of a space extends to all of it, no bigger | Extend a bounded functional from a subspace to the whole space keeping its norm. |
| · | Separation: two convex sets that miss each other can be split by a flat wall | Separate a point from a convex set with one functional and read off the supporting wall. |
| · | Three Banach theorems: completeness turns weak checks into full continuity | Use Baire's theorem to make an inverse bounded, a graph closed and a family uniformly bounded. |
| · | Dual spaces: the continuous measurements on a space, and what they turn out to be | Identify the dual of lp, of Lp and of C on an interval, and pair an operator with its transpose. |
| · | Reflexive spaces: when a space is exactly the dual of its own dual | Test reflexivity and say which bounded sequences are promised a weakly convergent subsequence. |

</details>

*inner products and angles; projection; orthonormal expansions and Parseval; representation of functionals; kernels; weak limits*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Inner products: length and angle in a space of signals | Use Cauchy-Schwarz, and test whether a given norm secretly comes from an inner product. |
| · | Projection: every point has exactly one nearest point in a closed subspace | Split a vector into a part inside a subspace and a perpendicular part, and check idempotence. |
| · | Orthonormal bases: every signal is an infinite sum of perpendicular pieces | Expand a function in an orthonormal basis and check that energy in equals energy out. |
| · | Completeness of sines and cosines: Fourier series leave nothing behind | Show no square-integrable signal is perpendicular to every harmonic, so the expansion is exact. |
| · | Orthogonal polynomials: straightening the powers into a perpendicular family | Run Gram-Schmidt on 1, x and x squared in L2, then use the result to fit and to integrate. |
| · | Riesz representation: every measurement on a Hilbert space is one fixed vector | Turn a bounded functional into an inner product with a single vector, and back again. |
| · | Lax-Milgram: a bounded energy form that stays away from zero has one solution | Write a boundary problem as a form and check the two conditions that give a unique solution. |
| · | Reproducing kernels: a space where reading off a value is an inner product | Build the space of a kernel, then write the best fit as a sum of kernels at the data points. |
| · | Weak convergence: only the measurements must settle, and that buys compactness | Extract a weakly convergent subsequence from a bounded sequence and say what is lost. |

</details>

*operator norm and continuity; the model operators; adjoints; compactness; spectrum and resolvent; the two spectral theorems*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Bounded operators: for a linear map, no blow-up is the same as continuity | Compute an operator norm, bound outputs with it, and check that operators form a complete space. |
| · | Three model operators: integrate against a kernel, multiply, shift | Write each as an operator, compute its norm, and see which matrix intuitions survive. |
| · | Adjoints: the mirror operator, and the classes it singles out | Compute an adjoint, then tell self-adjoint, unitary, normal and positive operators apart. |
| · | Plancherel: the Fourier transform is a rigid rotation of the space of signals | Check that the transform preserves total energy, then extend it from nice functions by density. |
| · | Compact operators: the maps that still behave like finite matrices | Show a kernel operator is compact as a limit of finite-rank ones, and use the closed-ideal rule. |
| · | Neumann series: a geometric series that inverts an operator near the identity | Solve x equal to b plus Kx by repeating K when K is small, and bound the error after n terms. |
| · | Spectrum: the numbers at which an operator cannot be inverted | Compute a spectrum containing no eigenvalues, and split it into point, continuous and residual parts. |
| · | Compact and symmetric: an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, eigenvalues dying away | Diagonalise a symmetric kernel operator and read its largest eigenvalue off a ratio. |
| · | Fredholm alternative: either always solvable, or finitely many obstructions | Decide whether an integral equation is solvable by checking the adjoint's null space. |
| · | Bounded and symmetric: diagonal against a family of projections, not eigenvectors | Write a symmetric operator as multiplication by a function, then apply functions to the operator. |

</details>

| · | Sobolev and Poincare: trading derivative control for size and smoothness | Bound a function by its derivative, and say when a weak solution is genuinely continuous. |
| · | Boundary values and compactness: the two Sobolev results existence proofs lean on | State what a trace and a compact embedding give you, and where each one is used. |

<a name="w19"></a>

## 19 · Partial differential equations

Classify a second-order equation, say whether its problem is well posed, and solve the model equations by characteristics, kernels, Green's functions and eigenfunction expansions. Say what a weak or a viscosity solution is, set up a free boundary problem such as an American option, and read the Navier-Stokes regularity question as a precise statement.

**62 cards in 7 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Classification and Well-Posedness</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*What a PDE problem is, the three families, and what it takes for a problem to be well posed*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | A PDE problem: the equation, the region, and the data that pin it down | State a PDE problem in full: order, region, initial data, boundary data, and what counts as a solution. |
| · | Linear or not: when you may add two solutions together | Grade an equation linear, semilinear, quasilinear or fully nonlinear, and use superposition to build solutions from pieces. |
| · | Boundary conditions: fix the value, fix the flow, or mix the two | Write Dirichlet, Neumann, Robin and periodic conditions for one plate and check the Neumann compatibility condition. |
| · | Elliptic, parabolic, hyperbolic: three families with three behaviours | Classify a second-order equation from its principal part and predict whether its solutions settle, spread or travel. |
| · | Canonical form: change coordinates until you recognise the equation | Change variables so a general second-order equation becomes the plain wave, heat or Laplace equation. |
| · | Well posed: a solution exists, is unique, and does not amplify errors | State Hadamard's three demands and show the backward heat equation and Hadamard's own example fail the third. |
| · | Energy methods: multiply by the solution and integrate | Prove uniqueness and stability for the heat and wave equations from an energy estimate and Gronwall's inequality. |
| · | Maximum principles: the extreme values sit on the boundary | Prove the weak maximum principle for Laplace's equation and use the comparison principle to bound a solution. |
| · | Dependence and influence: which data reaches a point, and how far it spreads | Draw the dependence and influence cones for the wave equation and contrast them with diffusion's instant reach. |

</details>

| · | Weak solutions: multiply by a test function and integrate by parts | Turn a PDE into its weak form, check that a kinked function solves it, and watch the smoothness demand drop. |
| · | Sobolev spaces: the right home for a solution of limited smoothness | Set up H1 and its zero-boundary version, and state the trace theorem and Poincare inequality a PDE needs. |
| · | Lax-Milgram: one weak solution when the form is bounded and coercive | Check boundedness and coercivity for a second-order elliptic problem and conclude existence and uniqueness. |
| · | Galerkin: solve the weak form on finitely many chosen shapes | Assemble a finite element system from hat functions and bound its error by Cea's lemma. |
| · | Elliptic regularity: the weak solution turns out to be smooth after all | State interior and boundary regularity, and see why smooth data forces a smooth solution. |
| · | Viscosity solutions: test with smooth functions that touch the graph | Define viscosity sub- and supersolutions, check a kinked example, and state the comparison principle that gives uniqueness. |
| · | Free boundaries: when the edge of the region is part of the answer | Set up a problem whose interface is unknown and move that interface by the Stefan condition. |
| · | The obstacle problem: a membrane that must clear an obstacle | State the obstacle problem as a variational inequality and find the contact set and its free boundary. |
| · | An American option: the boundary where holding stops beating exercising | Write the American problem as a variational inequality, impose value matching and smooth pasting, and locate the boundary. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>07 · Nonlinear PDE and Fluids</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*The fluid equations, the regularity question, patterns and solitons, and equations with the unknown under an integral*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Navier-Stokes: mass and momentum balance for a moving fluid | Derive the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations and say what job the pressure does in them. |
| · | Leray's weak solutions: they exist in three dimensions, but may not be unique | Set up the weak formulation of Navier-Stokes, state Leray's existence theorem, and say exactly what it fails to give. |
| · | The energy inequality, and the gap it leaves in three dimensions | Derive the energy inequality, see why two dimensions closes and three does not, and state the regularity problem precisely. |
| · | Vorticity: the spin form of the equations, and the zero-viscosity limit | Take the curl to get the vorticity equation, see vortex stretching in three dimensions, and compare Euler with Navier-Stokes. |
| · | Reaction-diffusion: spreading plus local growth makes fronts and patterns | Find a travelling front speed and see when a uniform state goes unstable into stripes or spots. |
| · | Solitons: when steepening and spreading balance exactly | See how the nonlinear Schrodinger and KdV equations hold a lump together, and what makes such equations special. |
| · | Integral equations: the unknown sits inside the integral | Convert a boundary value problem into an integral equation and tell the Fredholm kinds from the Volterra kind. |
| · | The Fredholm alternative: either exactly one solution, or extra free ones | Solve a second-kind equation by Neumann series, state the Fredholm alternative, and expand a kernel in its own modes. |
| · | When prices jump: a pricing equation with an integral term | Write the PIDE for a jump-diffusion, see why the integral term is non-local, and sketch how it is solved. |

</details>

<a name="w20"></a>

## 20 · Harmonic analysis

Prove the convergence theorems for Fourier series and the inversion and Plancherel theorems for the transform, and use convolution, kernels and approximate identities as everyday tools. Derive the probability results that rest on characteristic functions, build and use wavelets, and read a modern harmonic-analysis statement about maximal functions, singular integrals and restriction.

**52 cards in 6 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Fourier Series in Depth</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Coefficients, kernels, and exactly when and in what sense the series equals the function*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Fourier coefficients: each wave's share, read off by an integral that ignores the rest | Compute the sine, cosine and complex coefficients of a repeating signal, and prove the orthogonality that isolates each one. |
| · | Partial sums are the best fit: least squares in function space, and Bessel's inequality | Prove the partial sum beats every other trigonometric polynomial of its degree in mean square, and deduce Bessel's inequality. |
| · | Dirichlet and Fejer kernels: a partial sum is the signal smeared by a fixed shape | Write a partial sum and its running average as convolutions with closed-form kernels, and compare their sizes and signs. |
| · | Dirichlet's theorem: where the series equals the signal, and where it averages the jump | Prove pointwise convergence for piecewise smooth signals, get uniform convergence from summable coefficients, and state the localisation principle. |
| · | Convergence in energy: Parseval, completeness, and Riesz-Fischer | Prove mean-square convergence for any square-integrable signal, prove Parseval, and rebuild a signal from any square-summable coefficient list. |
| · | Smoothness shows in the tail: how fast coefficients shrink, and how many terms you need | Relate each extra derivative to one more power of decay, and predict the term count for a stated accuracy. |
| · | Differentiating a series term by term: when it is legal, and what integration costs | State and prove the conditions for differentiating and integrating a series one term at a time, and see a failure. |
| · | The Gibbs overshoot and the cure: Fejer averaging and half-range series | Compute the nine percent overshoot at a jump, remove it by averaging with Fejer's theorem, and build half-range series. |
| · | Carleson's theorem in outline: almost everywhere, the series does converge | State Carleson's theorem, contrast a continuous signal whose series diverges at a point, and say what each one settles. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · The Fourier Transform</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*The transform of an integrable and of a square-integrable signal, inversion, energy, distributions, and the limits on locality*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The Fourier transform of an absolutely integrable signal, and why it fades at infinity | Define the transform, prove it is bounded and continuous, fix one sign and scaling convention, and prove Riemann-Lebesgue. |
| · | The transform rulebook: shifting, scaling, and turning derivatives into multiplication | Prove the shift, modulation, scaling, duality and derivative rules, then transform a new signal without doing any integral. |
| · | Two pairs worth memorising: gate and sinc, and the bell curve that transforms to itself | Derive both pairs from the definition, one by completing the square, and use them as benchmarks for code. |
| · | Fourier inversion: rebuilding the signal from its spectrum, proved with a bell curve | Prove inversion by inserting a shrinking Gaussian factor, and say what goes wrong when the spectrum is not integrable. |
| · | Plancherel: the transform preserves energy, and extends to every square-integrable signal | Prove the energy identity on rapidly decreasing signals and extend the transform by density, getting a distance-preserving map. |
| · | Schwartz space and tempered distributions: transforms of spikes, constants and steps | Define rapidly decreasing test functions and their duals, then transform the delta, the constant and the sign function. |
| · | The uncertainty principle: no signal is both brief and narrow in frequency | Prove the Heisenberg-Gabor inequality with Cauchy-Schwarz, show the bell curve attains it, and state Paley-Wiener. |
| · | Poisson summation and sampling: samples are enough when nothing wiggles too fast | Prove Poisson summation, deduce the sampling theorem and sinc interpolation, and explain aliasing as overlapping spectral copies. |
| · | The Hilbert transform: shift every frequency a quarter turn, and read off the envelope | Define the transform by its multiplier and its principal-value kernel, build the analytic signal, and extract envelope and phase. |
| · | Transforms in two and three dimensions: a slice of the spectrum is a shadow of the object | Transform on the plane using separability and rotation invariance, and prove the projection-slice theorem behind medical scanning. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>03 · Convolution and Approximate Identities</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Convolution as filtering and as smoothing, the kernels that do it, and what it does to densities and to spectra*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Convolution: slide, multiply, add, and the inequality that bounds the result | Compute a convolution by hand, prove it commutes and associates, and prove Young's inequality bounding its size. |
| · | The convolution theorem: blending in time is multiplying in frequency | Prove the theorem both ways and read a time-invariant system as convolution with its response to one sharp kick. |
| · | Approximate identities: a shrinking bump that smooths a signal without moving it | Define an approximate identity, prove convergence in mean and at points of continuity, and deduce that smooth signals are dense. |
| · | The Gaussian and Poisson kernels: heat spreading, and a disc's boundary values | Check both families are approximate identities, recover the heat kernel and the Poisson integral, and compare their tails. |
| · | Blurring and unblurring: why a blur is easy and undoing it is not | Blur an image by a bell-shaped kernel, try dividing in frequency, watch the noise explode, and damp it. |
| · | Correlation: sliding one signal against another to find a known shape in noise | Compute autocorrelation and cross-correlation, tell them from convolution, and derive the matched filter as the best detector. |
| · | Wiener-Khinchin: the power spectrum is the transform of the autocorrelation | Define a stationary random signal and its power spectrum, prove the theorem, and show a filter multiplies it by gain squared. |
| · | Densities add by convolution: the law of a sum of independent quantities | Prove the density of a sum is the convolution, compute it for two uniforms and two bell curves, and check it in frequency. |
| · | Solving differential equations by transform: derivatives become multiplication | Solve the heat equation on a line and a forced oscillator by transforming, dividing and transforming back. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>04 · Characteristic Functions and Probability</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Fourier analysis of probability laws: inversion, continuity, the central limit theorem, jump laws and option pricing*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Characteristic functions: the transform of a probability law, which always exists | Compute fingerprints for the coin, uniform, bell and Cauchy laws, and prove the properties every fingerprint has. |
| · | Moments and cumulants: what differentiating the fingerprint at zero tells you | Get means and variances by differentiating, define cumulants from the logarithm, and read skewness and tail weight off them. |
| · | Levy's inversion formula: getting the law back out of its fingerprint | Prove the inversion formula for the distribution function, deduce that the fingerprint determines the law, and recover a density. |
| · | Levy's continuity theorem: when fingerprints converge, the laws converge too | Prove both directions with a tightness argument, and see the trap when the limit is not itself a fingerprint. |
| · | The central limit theorem in four lines, once you have fingerprints | Expand the fingerprint to second order, raise it to the n-th power, take the limit and recognise the bell curve. |
| · | How big must n be: the Berry-Esseen bound and the skewness correction | Bound the bell-curve approximation error with the Berry-Esseen constant, then add an Edgeworth term and measure the gain. |
| · | Infinitely divisible laws: the Levy-Khintchine recipe of drift, wobble and jumps | Recognise which laws split into n identical independent pieces, and read drift, variance and jump measure off the exponent. |
| · | Stable laws: the limits you reach when the variance is infinite | Define stability through the fingerprint, place the bell and Cauchy laws in the family, and state the generalised limit theorem. |
| · | Pricing by transform: Carr-Madan turns a fingerprint into a ladder of option prices | Damp the payoff, transform it, multiply by the model's fingerprint and invert once to price every strike together. |

</details>

| · | Continuous wavelet transform: compare the signal with stretched and shifted wiggles | Define the transform against a mother wavelet, check the condition that makes it invertible, and read a scalogram. |
| · | The Haar wavelet: averages and differences, and the ladder of coarse-to-fine views | Build the Haar basis by hand, prove it is orthonormal, and state the nested-subspace axioms behind every wavelet family. |

| · | Filter banks: when splitting and rejoining returns the signal unchanged | State the mirror-filter conditions, verify exact reconstruction on the Haar pair, and see which aliasing they cancel. |
| · | Daubechies wavelets: short, smooth, and blind to polynomial trends | Count vanishing moments, derive the four-tap Daubechies filter from its conditions, and predict how few coefficients a smooth signal needs. |
| · | Wavelet denoising: keep the big coefficients, throw the small ones away | Threshold coefficients by the universal rule, compare hard with soft shrinkage, and beat a moving average on a spiky record. |
| · | Compression by wavelets: why a few coefficients carry a whole picture | Keep the largest coefficients, plot error against how many you keep, and compare with a block cosine transform. |
| · | Frames in outline: redundant building blocks that still rebuild a signal stably | State the frame inequality, tell a frame from a basis, and state the obstruction to a well-focused time-frequency basis. |

| · | Restriction and Kakeya in outline: what a spectrum living on a sphere can tell you | State the restriction problem and the Kakeya needle problem, and say how a needle bound would bound a transform. |
