## 21 · Algebraic and analytic number theory

Prove the prime number theorem with an error term, state the Riemann hypothesis precisely and say exactly what is proved around it, follow Dirichlet's theorem and the sieve results, and do Galois theory. Compute in a ring of algebraic integers and read the statements of Fermat, Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer and abc as mathematics rather than folklore.

**72 cards in 8 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Arithmetic Functions Again</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Multiplicative functions and Dirichlet convolution, Mobius inversion, average orders, partial summation, and the first genuine bounds on the prime count*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Big O and twiddle: how to say 'about this size' so the claim can be proved | Read and manipulate big O, little o and asymptotic equality, and say exactly what an error term claims. |
| · | Multiplicative functions: rules that split across coprime parts, and how to combine them | Recognise a multiplicative function, rebuild it from its prime-power values, and compute a Dirichlet convolution. |
| · | The Mobius function: plus one, minus one or zero, and how it undoes a divisor sum | Invert a sum over divisors to recover the original function, proving the inversion from convolution. |
| · | The von Mangoldt function: weighting prime powers by logs so the sums behave | Move between prime counts and log-weighted counts, and read the log derivative of zeta as a Dirichlet series. |
| · | Average orders: the typical divisor count, found by summing along a hyperbola | Compute the average order of the divisor and totient functions, with the error term Dirichlet's trick gives. |
| · | Partial summation: trading a weighted sum for an integral against a running total | Apply Abel's identity in both directions to pass between prime counts and weighted prime sums. |
| · | Squarefree numbers: why six in every ten integers are divisible by no square | Count squarefree numbers with a Mobius sieve and identify the density as six over pi squared. |
| · | Chebyshev's bounds: trapping the prime count between two multiples of x over log x | Prove upper and lower bounds on the prime count from central binomial coefficients, then deduce Bertrand's postulate. |
| · | Mertens' theorems: the reciprocals of the primes add up like log log x | Prove the three Mertens estimates and use them to size any product taken over the primes. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · The Zeta Function and the Prime Number Theorem</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*Zeta as an analytic object, its functional equation and zero-free region, Perron's formula, the prime number theorem with an error term, and what the Riemann hypothesis would add*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Zeta of a complex variable: the Euler product, the pole at one, and the log derivative | Prove the Euler product in its half-plane, locate the pole, and write the log derivative as a sum over prime powers. |
| · | The functional equation: zeta at s against zeta at one minus s, proved | Build the completed zeta function, prove the reflection formula, and locate the trivial zeros at the negative even integers. |
| · | The zero-free region: why zeta cannot vanish on the edge of the critical strip | Prove non-vanishing on the line where the real part is one by the three-four-one trick, then quote the widened region. |
| · | Perron's formula: a running total recovered from its series by a contour integral | Write a summatory function as a contour integral and bound the truncation error honestly. |
| · | The prime number theorem: the full proof, from zeta's zeros to the count x over log x | Assemble the contour shift, the zero-free region and partial summation into a complete proof with an error term. |
| · | The logarithmic integral: a far closer fit than x over log x, and where it too fails | Compare the prime count with Li(x) and with x over log x, and say what the classical error term does and does not allow. |
| · | The explicit formula: the prime count written exactly, one wave per zero of zeta | Derive von Mangoldt's explicit formula and see each zero contribute an oscillation to the prime count. |
| · | Counting the zeros: how many lie below a given height, and how tightly they pack | Use the Riemann-von Mangoldt formula to count zeros up to a height and estimate the spacing between them. |
| · | The Riemann hypothesis: the exact statement, and three equivalents worth knowing | State the hypothesis precisely and show it equivalent to the sharp prime error term and to a bound on the Mertens function. |
| · | Zeros on the line: what is proved, from Hardy to a positive proportion | State Hardy's theorem, Selberg's positive proportion and the Levinson and Conrey fractions, plus the verified numerical range. |

</details>

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Dirichlet characters: periodic multiplicative patterns that pick out one remainder class | List the characters to a modulus and use orthogonality to isolate a single residue class inside a sum. |
| · | Quadratic residues: which numbers are squares on a prime clock, and Euler's test | Decide whether a number is a square modulo a prime using Euler's criterion, and read the Legendre symbol as a character. |
| · | Dirichlet L-functions: one series per character, each with its own Euler product | Build an L-function, prove its Euler product, and see why non-vanishing at one is the whole difficulty. |
| · | Dirichlet's theorem: a progression coprime to its step holds infinitely many primes | Prove infinitude of primes in a progression from non-vanishing at one, and state the equal-share density version. |
| · | Siegel-Walfisz: the prime count in a progression, uniform over small moduli | State the theorem, see what an exceptional zero would cost, and know why the modulus must stay small. |
| · | Gauss sums: weighted sums over roots of unity, with a size you can pin down exactly | Evaluate a quadratic Gauss sum, prove its absolute value is root p, and fix its sign. |
| · | Quadratic reciprocity: swapping the two primes, proved with a Gauss sum | Prove the reciprocity law from Gauss sums and use it to settle a square-or-not question in seconds. |
| · | The Jacobi symbol: extending the symbol to odd moduli so it computes without factoring | Compute a Jacobi symbol by reciprocity alone, and see how Solovay-Strassen turns it into a primality test. |

</details>

<summary><b>04 · Sieves and Prime Gaps</b> · 8 cards</summary>

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Sieving by inclusion-exclusion: Legendre's count, and the error that swamps it | Run the Legendre sieve, watch the error terms overwhelm the main term, and name the parity obstruction. |
| · | Brun's sieve and Selberg's: truncating inclusion-exclusion so the error stays small | Bound the count of twin primes below x by Brun's sieve, and state Selberg's optimised weights. |
| · | The large sieve: how concentrated a set can be across many remainder classes at once | State the large sieve inequality and use it to bound a set that avoids many residue classes. |
| · | Bombieri-Vinogradov: primes spread evenly across classes, on average over the moduli | State the theorem as a usable stand-in for the Riemann hypothesis for L-functions, and name the range of moduli it covers. |
| · | Bounded gaps: from pairs within 70 million to pairs within 246 | Follow the GPY weights and Maynard's sieve in outline, and state exactly what is proved about small gaps. |
| · | Large gaps: the longest prime-free run, and the coin-flip model that predicts it | Compute record gaps, compare them with the Cramer heuristic, and state the best proved lower bound. |
| · | Goldbach: every even number as two primes, and how far that is actually proved | State the binary and ternary conjectures, then quote Helfgott's ternary theorem and Chen's semiprime result. |
| · | Landau's four problems: the questions about primes that are all still open | State Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre's conjecture and n squared plus one, and say what the sieves give for each. |

</details>

| · | Class groups: measuring how badly factorisation fails, and how far you must search | Compute a class number using the Minkowski bound, and read the class group as the obstruction to unique factorisation. |
| · | The class number formula: an analytic limit that counts the failure of factorisation | Write the Dedekind zeta function of a field and read its class number off the residue at one. |
| · | The unit theorem: how many independent invertible elements a ring of integers holds | Count the independent units from the real and complex embeddings, and find a fundamental unit. |
| · | Splitting and ramification: what becomes of an ordinary prime in a bigger ring | Decide whether a prime splits, stays inert or ramifies, and tie the ramified primes to the discriminant. |

<a name="w22"></a>

## 22 · Algebraic geometry

Compute with polynomial systems: choose a term order, run Buchberger's algorithm, eliminate variables, and count the solutions of a finite system. Read a variety in affine and projective space, work with a plane curve and its singularities, do arithmetic on an elliptic curve up to the statements of Mordell-Weil and Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer, and state the Hodge conjecture precisely.

**54 cards in 6 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Polynomial Systems</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Ideals, term orders, Grobner bases, elimination, and the two theorems that make polynomial algebra finite*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Ideals and varieties: the equations you may add, and the points that survive them | Write the ideal generated by a system, see that every combination vanishes on the same points, and name the variety. |
| · | Monomial orders: ranking terms so that division in several variables has an answer | Compare lex, graded lex and grevlex on the same terms, then divide one polynomial by a list and read the remainder. |
| · | Grobner bases: the generating set that makes division give a definite answer | Form S-polynomials, run Buchberger's algorithm by hand on two polynomials, and apply the criterion that says you are finished. |
| · | Normal forms: deciding whether one equation is a consequence of the others | Reduce a basis to its unique tidy form, test membership by dividing down to zero, and compare two systems for equality. |
| · | Elimination: removing variables to solve a system or to find a curve's equation | Use a lex basis to remove variables one at a time, extend a partial solution back, and find a parametrised curve's equation. |
| · | The resultant: one determinant that says when two polynomials share a root | Build the Sylvester matrix of two polynomials, read off when they share a root, and eliminate a variable without a basis. |
| · | Finitely many solutions: counting them first, then finding them all | Show a system is zero-dimensional, count solutions from the quotient's dimension, and find them as eigenvalues of multiplication matrices. |
| · | Hilbert's basis theorem: every polynomial system, however large, is really a finite one | Prove the monomial case by Dickson's lemma, deduce the general theorem, and see why Buchberger's algorithm must halt. |
| · | The Nullstellensatz: over the complex numbers the points know the equations up to roots | State the weak and the strong form, certify that a system has no solution, and match radical ideals with varieties. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · Affine and Projective Varieties</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Varieties in affine and projective space, the maps between them, dimension, tangent spaces and smoothness*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Affine varieties: the zero set of a system, and its ring of polynomial functions | Name the variety of a system, restrict polynomials to it, and read the coordinate ring as polynomials modulo the vanishing ideal. |
| · | The Zariski topology: calling solution sets closed, and splitting a shape into pieces | List the closed sets of the line and the plane, split a variety into irreducible pieces, and match those pieces with prime ideals. |
| · | Projective space: adding points at infinity so that parallel lines meet | Write points as ratios that rescaling does not change, see the plane plus its line at infinity, and work in the standard charts. |
| · | Projective varieties: equations of one degree, and the closure of an affine shape | Homogenise a system, find the points it gains at infinity, and dehomogenise back into each chart without losing information. |
| · | Maps between varieties: polynomial maps, and ratios with a bad set | Write a regular map and its effect on coordinate rings, then find exactly where a rational map is undefined. |
| · | Birational equivalence: the same away from a small bad set, and the field that detects it | Build the function field of a variety and show two varieties are birational by exhibiting inverse rational maps. |
| · | Dimension and tangent space: how many directions a shape has at a point | Compute dimension by transcendence degree and by chains of primes, then get the tangent space by linearising the equations. |
| · | Smooth and singular points: the Jacobian test for a clean tangent direction | Apply the Jacobian criterion to a surface, find its singular points, and check a curve for smoothness from its partial derivatives. |
| · | Degree: how many points a slice meets, and the polynomial that records it | Count where a projective variety meets a generic complementary plane, and read degree and dimension off its Hilbert polynomial. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>03 · Plane Curves</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*Curves in the plane: how they meet, how they fail to be smooth, how many holes they have, and the functions on them*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Conics: every smooth degree-two curve is a line in disguise | Classify a conic from its matrix, then parametrise it from one rational point and generate every other rational point on it. |
| · | Intersection multiplicity: how tightly two curves touch where they meet | Compute the multiplicity of a crossing from a resultant or a local ring, and see why a tangency counts as two. |
| · | Bezout's theorem: two curves meet in exactly the product of their degrees | Count the intersections of a cubic with a line and of two cubics, and see why projective space and multiplicity are both needed. |
| · | Nodes and cusps: the standard ways a curve fails to be smooth | Read multiplicity and tangent cone at a bad point, tell a node from a cusp, and classify by the lowest-degree terms. |
| · | Blowing up: replacing a point by all the directions through it to pull branches apart | Blow up the origin to separate the two branches of a node, and repeat the move until a plane curve is smooth. |
| · | Genus: the number of holes, and how degree and singular points fix it | Get the genus of a smooth plane curve from its degree, subtract the delta invariant of each singular point, and test for rationality. |
| · | Divisors and Riemann-Roch: counting the functions with the poles you prescribe | Add up zeros and poles as a divisor, test two divisors for linear equivalence, and apply Riemann-Roch on a genus-one curve. |
| · | Riemann surfaces: a complex curve is a surface, and a doughnut is a rolled-up plane | See a complex cubic as a torus, build the Weierstrass p-function from a lattice, and recover the cubic equation from it. |
| · | Inflections and Cayley-Bacharach: nine points on a cubic, and the ninth for free | Find the inflections of a cubic with the Hessian, and prove that a cubic through eight of nine crossings passes through the ninth. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>04 · Elliptic Curves</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*The group law on a cubic, arithmetic over Q and over finite fields, the two big theorems, and the cryptography*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The group law: three points on a line add up to zero | Add and double points by chord and tangent, use the point at infinity as zero, and check associativity against Cayley-Bacharach. |
| · | Weierstrass form: the standard cubic, its discriminant and its j-invariant | Reduce a cubic to short Weierstrass form, test smoothness with the discriminant, and identify two curves by the j-invariant. |
| · | Adding points by formula: slopes, the doubling case, and big multiples fast | Implement the slope formulas including doubling, then compute a large multiple by double-and-add in projective coordinates. |
| · | Torsion points: the ones that return to zero, and how to list them all | Bound the candidates by Nagell-Lutz, check them with division polynomials, and read Mazur's list of the fifteen possibilities. |
| · | The curve mod p: counting its points, and Hasse's bound on the count | Count points over a small prime field by hand, check the count against Hasse's bound, and find the primes of bad reduction. |
| · | Mordell-Weil: every rational point comes from a finite list of them | State the theorem, split the group into torsion and rank, and follow a descent far enough to find one generator. |
| · | The L-function: every prime's point count packed into one series | Build the local factor at a good prime and at a bad one, assemble the Hasse-Weil L-function, and say where its product converges. |
| · | Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer: the rank equals the order of vanishing at s equals 1 | State the weak and strong forms exactly, name every term in the formula, and list what is proved for rank 0 and rank 1. |
| · | Curves for keys: the discrete logarithm problem, and counting points to pick a safe curve | Run a key exchange and a signature on a small curve, then see how Schoof's point count and the known attacks set key sizes. |
| · | What curves answer: congruent numbers, and the modularity theorem | Turn the congruent number question into rational points on a curve, and state modularity and what it settled. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>05 · Cohomology and the Hodge Conjecture</b> · 8 cards</summary>

*Cohomology in outline for varieties, the Hodge decomposition, cycle classes, and one Clay problem stated exactly*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Sheaves: data that exists locally, glued together with bookkeeping | Define a sheaf by its sections over open sets, restrict and glue over a cover, and see why global sections can be scarce. |
| · | De Rham cohomology: closed forms modulo exact ones, counted degree by degree | Compute the first cohomology of the punctured plane, and read a class as a closed form with no antiderivative. |
| · | Cohomology of projective space: one class in every even degree | List the Betti numbers of complex projective space, identify the class of a hyperplane, and multiply two classes by intersecting. |
| · | Complex manifolds and Kahler forms: holomorphic charts and a metric that fits them | Split coordinates into holomorphic and conjugate parts, write forms in dz and dz-bar, and check the Kahler condition on projective space. |
| · | Hodge decomposition: cohomology of a compact Kahler manifold splits by type | Split the k-th cohomology into pieces of type p and q, check the symmetry, and do it for a torus. |
| · | Line bundles and divisors: a codimension-one shape as a cohomology class | Turn a divisor into a line bundle and a class in the second cohomology, then compute the Picard group of the projective plane. |
| · | Algebraic cycles: subvarieties added with weights, and the classes they leave behind | Form a cycle from subvarieties with integer weights, take its cohomology class, and check that the class has type p and p. |
| · | The Hodge conjecture: every rational class of type p and p comes from a cycle | State the conjecture with its rational coefficients, give the Lefschetz case of type (1, 1), and note the failure over the integers. |

</details>

| | Card | You'll be able to |

| · | The spectrum of a ring: prime ideals as points, so every ring becomes a space | List the primes of the integers and of a polynomial ring, give them the Zariski topology, and say what a scheme adds. |
| · | Localisation: inverting chosen functions to zoom in on one point | Invert a set of elements formally, build the local ring at a point, and say which geometry that ring sees. |
| · | Morphisms of schemes: ring maps read backwards, and fibres as products | Read a ring homomorphism as a map of spectra, form a fibre product, and watch a family of curves degenerate over one point. |
| · | Modules: vector spaces over a ring, and the classification when the ring is a PID | Define module, submodule and quotient, then decompose a finitely generated module over a PID into free and cyclic torsion parts. |
| · | Smith normal form: reducing an integer matrix until the module is readable off it | Row and column reduce an integer matrix to diagonal form, read the invariant factors, and get a matrix's rational canonical form. |
| · | Moduli spaces: a space whose points are the shapes of a given kind | See the j-invariant as the moduli space of elliptic curves, and the Grassmannian as the space of lines in space. |
| · | Algebraic statistics: a model is a variety, identifiability a question about ideals | Write a small model as a polynomial map of its parameters, eliminate them to get the model's equations, and test identifiability. |
| · | Tropical geometry and computation: curves become polygons, and bases can blow up | Read a tropical curve off a Newton polygon, and know where Grobner computations become infeasible and which software to reach for. |

## 23 · Differential geometry and Lie groups

Compute the curvature of a curve or a surface by hand and in code, work on a smooth manifold with vector fields, differential forms and Stokes' theorem, and follow Riemannian curvature far enough to state Ricci flow and the Einstein equations. Use a Lie group as a symmetry, optimise on a curved constraint set, and read the Yang-Mills mass-gap problem as a precise statement rather than a slogan.

**69 cards in 7 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Curves</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*how a path bends and twists, and why bending alone fixes its shape*

| · | Osculating circle: the circle that hugs a curve best | Reader finds the radius and centre of the best-fitting circle at a point. |
| · | Frenet-Serret frame: the axes a traveller carries along the curve | Reader builds the tangent, normal and binormal at a point and differentiates the frame. |
| · | Torsion: how fast a curve twists out of its own plane | Reader computes torsion and checks that constant curvature plus constant torsion means a helix. |
| · | Curvature and torsion at any speed: formulas that skip relabelling | Reader gets both numbers straight from raw position data without reparametrising. |
| · | Fundamental theorem of curves: bending and twisting fix the shape | Reader rebuilds a space curve from prescribed curvature and torsion by solving the frame equations. |
| · | Turning and winding numbers: whole turns a closed loop makes | Reader counts turns of the tangent and wraps around a point, and sees both survive deformation. |
| · | Isoperimetric and four-vertex: two theorems the circle wins | Reader proves the circle encloses the most area for a given length and finds four curvature extremes. |

## 24 · Computability and complexity

Define computation precisely, prove that some problems cannot be solved at all and others cannot be solved fast under standard assumptions, and read P versus NP as a precise statement with a clear account of what is proved around it. Follow randomised, quantum and interactive models well enough to say what each one buys, and read category-theoretic language when it appears.

**57 cards in 7 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · Models of Computation</b> · 10 cards</summary>

*machines with fixed memory, with a stack and with a tape; what computation means; the first impossibility results*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Finite automata: fixed memory reads once and decides accept or reject | Draw a state machine for a pattern and say which patterns fixed memory can handle. |
| · | Guessing machines: allowed every move at once, and no more powerful | Turn a guessing machine into a deterministic one by tracking the set of possible states. |
| · | Regular expressions: patterns and machines describe the same string sets | Compile a pattern into a machine, run it on text, and read the machine back as a pattern. |
| · | Pumping and Myhill-Nerode: proving fixed memory is not enough | Prove a pattern needs unbounded memory, and build the smallest machine when it does not. |
| · | Grammars and a stack: machines that handle nesting | Write a grammar for a nested structure and run the matching stack machine on it. |
| · | The language ladder: more memory buys strictly more patterns | Place a pattern on the ladder from fixed memory to full tape and justify the rung. |
| · | Turing machines: a tape, a head and a table of rules | Run a tape machine by hand and in code, and read its rule table as a program. |
| · | Church-Turing thesis: every reasonable machine computes the same things | Do one job in three models and argue that no reasonable model computes more. |
| · | Universal machines: one runs them all, and cannot predict them | Build an interpreter, then prove no program decides whether another program stops. |
| · | Rice's theorem: no interesting property of a program is decidable | Prove a new question undecidable by converting the stopping question into it. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>05 · Algebraic, Interactive and Quantum</b> · 7 cards</summary>

*gates and depth, counting rather than deciding, permanent against determinant, proof by conversation or spot check, and quantum circuits*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Boolean circuits: cost measured in gates and in depth | Build a circuit for a small function and compare its size and depth with a formula. |
| · | Counting: how many solutions, not merely whether one exists | See why counting can be harder than deciding, and name the class that counting defines. |
| · | Permanent against determinant: two near-identical formulas, wildly different cost | Compute both on one small matrix and read the gap as an algebraic P versus NP. |
| · | Interactive proofs: convincing a sceptic by conversation, or by spot checks | Run a two-party protocol for a hard claim and state what IP equals PSPACE and PCP buy. |
| · | Qubits: a unit vector of amplitudes, read once and collapsed | Write a two-qubit state, apply one gate, and compute the measurement probabilities. |
| · | BQP: what a quantum circuit does and does not speed up | Build a small quantum circuit, define its class, and say what it is not known to solve. |
| · | Shor and Grover: period finding breaks RSA, search gains a square root | Factor a small number by period finding and search an unsorted list in root-n steps. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>06 · Data, Learning and Fine-Grained Complexity</b> · 6 cards</summary>

*one-pass summaries, random projection, bits across a gap, how many examples are enough, regret, and hardness inside P*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Streaming: one pass, tiny memory, an answer with error bars | Summarise a stream in memory far below its size and state the accuracy that buys. |
| · | Random projection: squash the data, keep the distances | Project high-dimensional data down at random and bound how far distances move. |
| · | Communication complexity: how many bits must cross the gap | Bound the bits two parties must exchange, and turn that into a memory lower bound. |
| · | Learning with a guarantee: how many examples are enough | Bound the examples a hypothesis family needs, using its VC dimension. |
| · | Regret: judged against the best single choice in hindsight | Run a weighted-majority rule and bound its regret against the best fixed expert. |
| · | Fine-grained complexity: why quadratic is probably the floor | Use a strengthened hardness assumption to argue a known quadratic bound cannot be beaten. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>07 · Category Theory in Outline</b> · 9 cards</summary>

*categories and functors, naturality, universal properties, adjunctions, monoids and monads, folds, and side-by-side composition*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Categories and functors: objects, arrows, and maps that respect them | Check the axioms on two examples and build a functor from one to the other. |
| · | Commutative diagrams: every route agrees, and every arrow can be reversed | Read a diagram as equations, then dualise a statement by reversing all its arrows. |
| · | Natural transformations and Yoneda: an object is known by its arrows | Check a naturality square, then state Yoneda as knowing an object by the maps into it. |
| · | Universal properties: defined by the one arrow that always exists | Define an object by a universal property and prove it unique up to unique isomorphism. |
| · | Pullbacks and pushouts: matching pairs, and gluing along a shared part | Compute a pullback in sets and read a pushout as gluing two pieces along an overlap. |
| · | Adjunctions: two functors whose arrows correspond exactly | Read a free-and-forgetful pair as an adjunction and use it to move a problem across. |
| · | Monoids and monads: combining values, and sequencing steps that can fail | Fold a list with a monoid, chain failing lookups with a monad, and check the laws. |
| · | F-algebras: one step of a recursive structure, then folded away | Write a data type as a functor and replace its recursion by a single fold. |
| · | Monoidal categories: things side by side, drawn as parallel wires | Draw a process as a string diagram and read tensor and composition straight off it. |

</details>

---

<a name="w25"></a>

## 25 · Frontier

State each open problem exactly, say what is proved around it and what is not, tell a claim from an accepted proof from a formalisation, and follow a route of earlier cards to the point where the research literature starts. Nothing here promises that reading equips anyone to solve a problem; it promises the reader will know precisely what the problem is.

**43 cards in 7 shelves · planned**

<details>
<summary><b>01 · How Research Works</b> · 7 cards</summary>

*The habits of research mathematics: proof, conjecture, counterexample, statement, paper, machine, and how to start on an open problem*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Proof: a general argument, not a pile of checked cases | Tell a proof from evidence, see a conjecture that held to 10 to the 18 and then failed, and say what a computer check does and does not establish. |
| · | Kinds of claim: theorem, conjecture, model, approximation, and the status each carries | Label ten statements from the library by kind and by status, and see why the two labels are separate fields. |
| · | Counterexamples: one failure refutes a universal claim, and a million successes prove nothing | Construct counterexamples to three plausible claims and see the quantifier structure that makes one enough. |
| · | Reading a statement: quantifiers, domain, hypotheses, conclusion, and the boundary cases | Parse three research statements into their parts and test each on a small case. |
| · | Reading a paper: find the theorem, compare it with the abstract, trace what it depends on | Work through one short published paper, write its dependency list, and record what it leaves open. |
| · | Machines in proofs: numerical evidence, symbolic computation, exhaustive search, and proof assistants | Separate the four kinds of computer help, read a short Lean proof, and say what a formalisation guarantees. |
| · | Working on a problem: small cases, the literature, the status ledger, and knowing when you are wrong | Set up a working file for one problem: exact statement, known results, small cases computed, ideas tried, and the date of each check. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>02 · The Millennium Problems</b> · 7 cards</summary>

*Each problem stated exactly, with its hypotheses, what it does not claim, what is known, and the route that reaches it*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The Riemann hypothesis: every nontrivial zero of zeta has real part one half | State the hypothesis, say what it would give for primes, and list what is proved: the critical strip, the zero-free region, the proportion on the line. |
| · | Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer: the rank of an elliptic curve equals the order of vanishing of its L-function | State the conjecture in both its weak and strong forms and list the cases proved (rank 0 and 1 by Gross-Zagier-Kolyvagin). |
| · | P versus NP: is checking a solution really easier than finding one | State the problem with the precise classes, explain why most believe P differs from NP, and list the barriers (relativisation, natural proofs, algebrisation). |
| · | Navier-Stokes: do smooth solutions of the fluid equations exist for all time in three dimensions | State the four versions of the Clay problem (A to D), say what Leray proved in 1934, and separate the whole-space and periodic cases. |
| · | The Hodge conjecture: which cohomology classes come from algebraic subvarieties | State the conjecture with the rational coefficients and the (p, p) condition, and give the cases known (Lefschetz (1, 1), divisors) and the failure with integer coefficients. |
| · | Yang-Mills existence and mass gap: build the quantum theory rigorously and show its lightest particle has positive mass | State the two demands, say what a rigorous quantum field theory means (the Wightman or Osterwalder-Schrader axioms), and what lattice computations suggest. |
| · | The Poincare conjecture, solved: a simply connected closed three-manifold is a sphere | State the conjecture, outline Perelman's Ricci-flow proof with surgery, and record the acceptance and the declined prize. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>03 · Primes and Numbers</b> · 7 cards</summary>

*The open and recently settled questions about primes and integers, and the ones with prizes attached*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Twin primes: infinitely many primes two apart, and the bounded gap of 246 that is proved | State the conjecture, the Hardy-Littlewood prediction, and the Zhang-Maynard-Polymath result of 246. |
| · | Goldbach: every even number above 2 is a sum of two primes | State the strong and weak forms, record Helfgott's proof of the weak form (2013) and Chen's theorem, and see the numerical verification's limits. |
| · | Landau's four problems: Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre, and primes of the form n squared plus 1 | State all four exactly (Legendre is primes between consecutive squares) and what is known for each. |
| · | The abc conjecture: when a plus b equals c, the radical of abc is rarely much smaller than c | State the conjecture with its epsilon, show what it implies (Fermat for large exponents, Szpiro), and record the disputed status of the claimed proof. |
| · | Beal's conjecture: A to the x plus B to the y equals C to the z with exponents above 2 forces a common factor | State the conjecture, connect it to Fermat, and record the AMS prize terms. |
| · | Fermat's last theorem, proved in 1995 and reported formalised in 2026: what a formalisation adds | State the theorem, outline the modularity route, and read the September 2026 formalisation report for what was checked and how. |
| · | Erdos problems: a database of hundreds, with prizes, and the ones a computer search can touch | Read the Erdos problem database, pick three problems (distinct distances, sunflowers, unit distances) and record their status and the methods that moved them. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>04 · Geometry and Combinatorics</b> · 6 cards</summary>

*Open problems you can draw, some recently moved by computer search*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The unit-distance problem: how many pairs of n points can be exactly one apart | State Erdos's question and bounds, and read the May 2026 report of an improved exponent, separating 'superlinear' from the older constructions. |
| · | Kakeya: a set containing a unit segment in every direction, and how small it can be | State the Kakeya conjecture, its measure-zero examples, the reported 2025 three-dimensional result, and its links to the restriction conjecture. |
| · | Colouring the plane: how many colours so that no two points at distance one share a colour | State the problem, prove the bounds 5 to 7 (de Grey 2018 for the lower bound), and see why the answer may depend on the axioms. |
| · | Cycle double cover: every bridgeless graph has cycles covering each edge exactly twice | State the conjecture, prove it for planar graphs, and record what is known. |
| · | Sunflowers and union-closed families: two set-system problems with recent progress | State the sunflower conjecture and the Alweiss-Lovett-Wu-Zhang bound, and the union-closed conjecture with Gilmer's 2022 constant. |
| · | Sphere packing, solved in dimensions 8 and 24: Viazovska's modular-form proof | State the packing problem, the E8 and Leech lattices, and outline the magic function proof. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>05 · Analysis and Dynamics</b> · 6 cards</summary>

*Open questions about equations and iterations, including the 2026 Navier-Stokes claim*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The September 2026 Navier-Stokes claim: forced finite-time blow-up for versions C and D | Read the announcement and paper links, separate the mathematical claim from the formal artifact and from prize or community status, and record the Clay index as 'active'. |
| · | Collatz: halve if even, triple and add one if odd, and always reach 1 | State the conjecture, compute trajectories, and record Tao's 2019 almost-all result and the verification bound. |
| · | The August 2026 zeta-zeros claim: a reported rise in the proportion of zeros on the line from 41.6 to 67.2 percent | State what a proportion result proves and does not, read the announcement, and place it against Levinson and Conrey. |
| · | The Jacobian conjecture: a polynomial map with constant nonzero Jacobian has a polynomial inverse | State the conjecture, check it in one variable, and record the reductions (degree 3, symmetric case). |
| · | Two algebra questions: is every group sofic, and is the sum of two nil left ideals nil (Kothe) | State both problems exactly with the definitions and record what is known. |
| · | Percolation: when does a random lattice connect, and the reported 'dying percolation' result to verify | State the percolation threshold problem, Kesten's one-half on the square lattice, and record the 2026 lead as unverified. |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>06 · Algebra, Logic and Computation</b> · 5 cards</summary>

*Open problems where algebra meets logic and computation*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | The inverse Galois problem: is every finite group the Galois group of some extension of the rationals | State the problem, prove it for abelian groups by cyclotomic fields, and record Shafarevich's solvable case. |
| · | The continuum hypothesis: no size between the integers and the reals, and why it cannot be settled | State the hypothesis, outline Godel's and Cohen's independence results, and say what 'independent' means for a working mathematician. |
| · | The unique games conjecture: one hardness assumption that would settle many approximation questions | State the conjecture, show what it implies for max-cut, and record the 2018 two-to-two result. |
| · | Graph isomorphism: are two drawings the same graph, and Babai's quasipolynomial algorithm | State the problem, its place between P and NP-complete, and Babai's 2015 result. |
| · | Hilbert's and Smale's lists: the problems that shaped a century, and the ones still open | Read both lists, mark each as solved, open or ill-posed, and pick the three most relevant to this library (Hilbert 8, 10, 16; Smale 3, 15, 17). |

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>07 · Physics, Finance and Machines</b> · 5 cards</summary>

*Where the frontier touches physics, finance and artificial intelligence*

| | Card | You'll be able to |
| :-: | --- | --- |
| · | Quantum advantage: what BQP can do that classical machines cannot, as far as anyone knows | State the class BQP, Shor's factoring as its flagship, and the current status of supremacy claims. |
| · | Machines that prove: what AI systems reported proving or formalising in 2026, and how to audit such a claim | Record the 2026 announcements (Fermat formalisation, zeta zeros, unit distances, Navier-Stokes), and write the audit list: statement, artifact, independent check, status. |
| · | Open questions in finance mathematics: rough volatility, the smile's true dynamics, and what a market model cannot know | State the rough-volatility evidence, the rough Bergomi model, and three open modelling questions. |
| · | Optimal transport: moving mass at least cost, and the theory questions behind modern learning | State the Monge and Kantorovich problems, compute a Wasserstein distance, and list what is open about why deep learning works. |
| · | The status register: every problem in this wing, its status, its date, and its source | Maintain the register: one row per problem, status from the fixed vocabulary, the date checked, the primary source, and the correction log. |

</details>
